## Character Motion Synthesis & Animation

- 2026-11-19, **A Tutorial on Complementarity-Based Contact Models for Robotics**, Onur Beker et.al., Paper: [@2611.26600](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26600)
> **Abstract:** Simulating rigid-body dynamics with contact is fundamental to robotics, from motion synthesis and control to mechanism design. Many widely used simulators rely on cone complementarity formulations, whose physical assumptions and numerical solution require substantial background. While excellent surveys, monographs, and course notes exist, they mostly address computational mechanics or computer graphics or document a single simulator. To the best of our knowledge, a self-contained pedagogical treatment for robotics, bringing these formulations and their solvers together with complete derivations and a unified notation, does not yet exist. This tutorial addresses this gap by connecting rigid-body dynamics, conic optimization, and frictional contact. In particular, we explain how complementarity conditions encode non-penetration and frictional dissipation, determining separation, sticking, and sliding without prescribed contact modes. We distinguish equivalent transcriptions from convex relaxations, showing how the latter can introduce artifacts such as lift-off and creep. We examine the existence and uniqueness of motion and contact forces, including the solver's role in selecting among multiple solutions. We then compare first-order and Newton-based methods, explain regularization and proximal strategies, and discuss trade-offs between computational cost, robustness, and physical fidelity. Connections to widely used simulators, optimal control, and differentiable simulation help readers interpret simulation results, choose suitable formulations and solvers, and develop their own contact dynamics routines.
- 2026-11-19, **Awaken-HI: Scaling Humanoid Intelligence with Whole-Body Human Experience**, EngineAI et.al., Paper: [@2611.26420](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26420)
> **Abstract:** A central goal of humanoid robotics is for robots to perform the everyday work people do in homes and workplaces. Existing egocentric video datasets cover many of these activities but provide little synchronized whole-body motion data for learning loco-manipulation at scale. We build Awaken-Human, which pairs approximately 50,000 hours of egocentric stereo video with synchronized whole-body human motion and covers more than 100 skills. Building on this, we also introduce Awaken-HI (Humanoid Intelligence), a vision-language-action model for humanoid loco-manipulation that jointly predicts original human motion and retargeted robot actions through a shared action expert. We show that loco-manipulation skills transfer more effectively from people to humanoids when human trajectories directly supervise the shared action expert. Increasing human pretraining data reduces held-out robot action prediction error according to an empirical scaling law and raises the mean success rate on downstream real-robot tasks. After full-corpus pretraining and T800 adaptation, Awaken-HI achieves a mean success rate of 93.75% across eight individual tasks and outperforms GR00T N1.7 and $π_{0.5}$ in both individual and long-horizon evaluations. The same policy generalizes to objects unseen during robot post-training and to new pairings of familiar objects and destinations.
- 2026-11-18, **CoRelate: Coordinated Human-Object Motion Synthesis through Interaction Relations**, Zhiwen Qiu et.al., Paper: [@2611.25166](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25166)
> **Abstract:** Human-object motion synthesis requires coordinated human participation as objects move toward specified goals. However, reaching object targets does not ensure that contact is established and maintained, while adjustments that improve contact may interfere with task completion. We introduce CoRelate, a diffusion framework that coordinates full-body human-object motion through explicit interaction relations and task feedback for rigid and articulated objects. We represent intended participation through hand-part relations that specify the participating hand, its part-relative reference position, and participation interval. These relations serve both as generation conditions and as references for measuring interaction realization. At each denoising step, a temporal refiner trained jointly with the generator combines relation deviations, task residuals, and motion features from the current prediction to jointly update human and object motion across the sequence. After generation, temporal motion correction addresses remaining contact failures by optimizing local motion segments while preserving object trajectories and protected human motion. Experiments demonstrate improved object-goal satisfaction, contact F1, and complete interaction success over the evaluated baselines. Ablation studies support the benefits of relation conditioning and the complementary roles of task and relation feedback.
- 2026-11-18, **Probing Physics in Motion Diffusion Models Against Their Own Estimate**, Saeed Ghorbani et.al., Paper: [@2611.24423](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24423)
> **Abstract:** Recent studies report that video diffusion models "know more than they show": physical properties decode better from their activations than from their inputs or outputs. But a diffusion model computes its clean-sample estimate from those same activations, so such a comparison measures how easily a readout extracts the information. Whether the model hides anything is a separate question. We set out to find hidden physical knowledge in kinematic text-to-motion diffusion models, where the physics of every intermediate estimate can be computed exactly, and recorded every layer along the sampling trajectories of MDM (two checkpoints) and MotionDiffuse. Against the usual baselines the layers do look ahead of the model's own estimate on balance and flight. With every readout tuned the same way, the estimate closes the gap: for balance and flight, adding a layer gains at most 0.01, a size that a positive control shows we would detect. The fall rate of jumps is the one clear exception. Early in denoising, MDM's layers add about 0.08 in $R^2$ (up to 0.11 in a refit) that the estimate does not yet show, and the gain fades as sampling proceeds; on MotionDiffuse it is small and not robust. The estimate also flags floating samples without training, but aborting them saves under a tenth of the compute. In a video model, plausibility that linear probes miss in Wan 2.1's clean latent becomes readable after one fixed nonlinearity.
- 2026-11-18, **SemPhys: Semantic-Physical Co-Learning for Humanoid Motion Generation via Differentiable World Models**, Zejian Kang et.al., Paper: [@2611.24074](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24074)
> **Abstract:** In human-robot collaboration, natural language lets people direct a humanoid's whole-body movements without programming joint trajectories. A common approach generates a kinematic reference from an instruction and relies on a tracking controller to execute it. However, as the controller maintains balance under contact constraints, the executed motion can depart from the reference in ways that change the action it conveys. Our central insight is that semantic alignment must be evaluated directly on the complete motion the robot physically executes. Building on this insight, we introduce \textbf{SemPhys}, a framework that trains motion generators using semantic feedback from simulated executions. A differentiable world model and a fixed tracking controller predict a short segment of a recorded execution. SemPhys inserts this prediction at its original position, scores the complete trajectory against the instruction, and backpropagates through the predicted segment. A paired anchoring loss stabilizes adaptation by keeping each generated reference close to its counterpart from the initial generator. On BONES-SEED, SemPhys largely preserves instruction retrieval accuracy from reference to execution and achieves the highest retrieval accuracy on executed motions among the evaluated methods.
- 2026-11-17, **Wear and Go: Calibration Free Sparse Inertial Motion Capture via Online Sensor Setup Estimation**, Yuanshuo Tan et.al., Paper: [@2611.22894](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22894)
> **Abstract:** Sparse inertial motion capture typically assumes that each sensor's anatomical identity and mounting orientation are known. We present MOSAIC (Motion-based Sensor Assignment and Inertial Calibration), a framework that recovers this setup from ordinary motion without a calibration pose. Our central insight is that body pose changes continuously while the sensor setup persists, allowing movements that are individually ambiguous to provide complementary evidence over time. A setup network processes unordered sensor streams with temporal and cross-sensor attention, then accumulates assignment and rotation evidence into a session-level estimate. The recovered setup canonicalizes measurements for frozen pretrained pose estimators, separating sensor configuration from motion reconstruction. For sensors worn at prescribed body sites, evaluation on real inertial recordings with randomized setups shows near-calibrated pose accuracy for bounded offsets and substantial benefits from accumulation under arbitrary orientations. Transfer across pose networks supports setup recovery as a reusable interface between interchangeable sensors and pretrained pose models.
- 2026-11-17, **ForceBody: Force-Paired Parametric Body Motion with Torque Uncertainty**, Joonwoo Kwon et.al., Paper: [@2611.22769](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22769)
> **Abstract:** Forces and torques drive human motion, central to biomechanics, robotics, and physics-aware animation, yet they are often absent from learning-based motion pipelines for body reconstruction and generation. Some motion datasets provide force annotations, but the paired motion sequences are incompatible with the parametric body models used in modern learning pipelines. In addition, existing datasets typically obtain joint torques through inverse dynamics, which is inherently sensitive to the quality of the input kinematics; however, they do not quantify the reliability of these torque estimates. We close this gap with ForceBody, the first dataset to bring ground truth force supervision into a parametric body representation, shipped with per-sample torque uncertainty. ForceBody pairs the SKEL body model with ground reaction forces and inverse-dynamics joint torques across 10,386 motion trials (9.7M frames, 26.9 hours) from 140 subjects. Each torque label is paired with a per-frame, per-joint uncertainty annotation obtained by Monte Carlo sampling through the inverse dynamics pipeline. We benchmark six architectures (MLP, Conv1D, LSTM, GRU, Mamba2, Transformer) on ground reaction force and joint torque prediction from SKEL motion. As one example use of the released uncertainty, finetuning a Transformer with per-sample uncertainty weighting reduces the MAE of joint torque estimation by 5%. Code and data are at https://github.com/DominoAI-Lab/ForceBody-NeurIPS-ED-2026.
- 2026-11-17, **MAVE: Reconstructing Editable 3D Blockouts from Film Shots**, Yifan Chang et.al., Paper: [@2611.22464](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22464)
> **Abstract:** Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Creators can change viewpoints and character paths, then render the edited scenes to condition video generation. Code is available at https://github.com/yfChang-cv/MAVE.
- 2026-11-16, **DuoFlow: Towards Synchronized Speech and Motion Generation with Fine-Grained Control**, Jun Dong et.al., Paper: [@2611.21530](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21530)
> **Abstract:** An embodied speaker's expressiveness depends not only on what is said, but also on how the voice sounds and how the body moves. However, existing joint speech-motion generation models are largely driven implicitly by transcript semantics or intermediate speech representations, offering little explicit control over motion hierarchy, body parts, or vocal style. To bridge this gap, we construct MotionSpeech, a structured benchmark for controllable joint speech-motion generation that includes transcripts, voice-style descriptions, and motion conditions at two hierarchical levels. Building on this benchmark, we propose DuoFlow, a dual-stream latent flow transformer that jointly denoises speech and motion under a shared noise level and a unified second-based temporal coordinate. Modality-specific residual connectors inject structured conditions into the corresponding streams, layer-wise bidirectional cross-modal attention coordinates their generation, and a monotonic phoneme pathway combines normalized progress anchors with CTC supervision to strengthen speech temporal modeling. DuoFlow provides a unified interface for fine-grained motion and vocal-style control and demonstrates the ability to synthesize high-quality, synchronized, and instruction-consistent speech-motion sequences. Project page, https://jundong-dev.github.io/DuoFlow-project-page.
- 2026-11-15, **SGIP: Learning Scene-Grounded Interaction Priors for Scene-Aware Human Motion Prediction**, Donghyeon Soon et.al., Paper: [@2611.20076](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20076)
> **Abstract:** Human motion prediction (HMP) aims to forecast future movement from an observed motion sequence, and recent methods have incorporated 3D scene geometry and object semantics to improve long-term plausibility. However, the destination of future motion remains ambiguous in cluttered scenes: the same observed motion can lead to different interaction targets or navigation modes. Existing methods either handle this ambiguity implicitly or rely on auxiliary signals like gaze that are hard to obtain in practice. To address this, we propose SGIP, which explicitly predicts where in the scene future motion will be grounded across both objects and open navigation regions, and uses it to condition future trajectory and full-body pose prediction. This scene-grounded interaction prior is learned via intent distillation: at training time only, a teacher model scores candidate scene regions by combining an LLM prompted with a textual description of the person's intent with geometric motion-scene features. A student model learns to reproduce these scores from only the observed motion and 3D scene, making intent and LLM unnecessary at test time. Across multiple benchmark datasets, SGIP outperforms prior scene-aware baselines on both trajectory and pose accuracy in a realistic deployment setting where neither gaze nor intent is available at inference, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The project page is available at https://sgip2026.github.io.
- 2026-11-14, **Spectral Steering: Local Inverse Geometry for Frozen Diffusion Generators**, Angelo Moroncelli et.al., Paper: [@2611.19572](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19572)
> **Abstract:** A frozen diffusion generator may need to change several output properties while preserving others. We study when guidance faithfully realizes such a structured request and whether local inverse correction improves its direction. Our account links this benefit to the interaction between the request and the objective-specific remaining-sampler Jacobian: conditioning alone is insufficient. Spectral steering combines a request-specific cosine-gain predictor with damped inverse correction and fixed-time relinearization for finite edits. Scalar nulls and matched-movement controls test the predicted mechanism. Across 8,923 instances in a retrospectively identified local regime, predicted and realized gains correlate at Spearman 0.79, versus -0.11 for condition number. Experiments span images, audio, robotics, and control and planning, including complex character motion generation. On 50 paired inpainting cases, four corrections improve unobserved-pixel PSNR over Adam by 3.25 dB at equal total derivative traversals. Together, the analysis and controls connect request-dependent geometry to directional gains and establish where these gains translate into downstream performance.
- 2026-11-14, **ImpedanceMimic: Learned Impedance as Action Space for Force-Accurate Motion Imitation**, Matthias Jammot et.al., Paper: [@2611.19153](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19153)
> **Abstract:** Recovering the forces behind a motion is ill-posed: different controllers can reproduce the same movement while generating different contact forces. Physics-based motion imitation resolves this ambiguity through simulation, but relies on fixed-gain proportional-derivative (PD) control. We show that fixed gains impose a trade-off between motion and force tracking, as contact and swing place conflicting demands on joint stiffness and damping. We introduce ImpedanceMimic, a reinforcement-learning policy that modulates per-joint stiffness, damping, and position setpoints from motion and force measurements, but requires only motion at inference. Across 129 participants and six activities, ImpedanceMimic outperforms matched fixed-gain PD under every integration scheme, direct-torque, muscle-actuated and existing imitation controllers in kinematic and force fidelity, approaching a dedicated motion-to-force regressor. By expressing gains as multiples of inertia-scaled priors, a single policy controls subject-specific biomechanical models without explicit anthropometric input and transfers zero-shot to unseen humans and a humanoid robot. The learned impedance forms a low-dimensional representation of motor state, shared across bodies, that generalizes to withheld activities: stiffness scales with load, and damping anticipates unloading.
- 2026-11-12, **Ropedia Xperience 2: A Gold Standard for Scaling Physical AI Environments and Behaviors**, Hao Li et.al., Paper: [@2611.17060](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17060)
> **Abstract:** Large foundation models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1 have recently been shown to control robots directly from visual observations. They nevertheless remain brittle on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks in real-world environments. Further progress depends on two complementary resources: demonstrations of human behavior that supervise how tasks are performed, and interactive environments in which policies can be rolled out, evaluated, and improved in closed loop. Language models have advanced along well-characterized scaling laws; the corresponding question for physical AI is how capability scales with environments and behaviors, which first requires identifying which data to scale and how to produce and reuse such data continuously. To address this question, we introduce Ropedia Xperience 2, comprising 1,589 released metric-scale 3D scans and a collection of 4,230 egocentric recordings of 74 tasks (131 hours). We further show that each capture can be repurposed for robot learning: scenes are converted into physically interactive simulation environments, human motion is retargeted to whole-body robot trajectories, and additional robot behaviors are obtained through virtual teleoperation and automated long-horizon trajectory generation. We propose this joint capture of environments and behaviors as a gold standard for physical AI data collection, laying a data foundation for establishing scaling laws over environments and behaviors.
- 2026-11-10, **EgoHMP: Achieving Precise Human Motion Prediction in 3D Scenes via Egocentric Cues**, Xin Zhao et.al., Paper: [@2611.13654](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13654)
> **Abstract:** The complex interactive dependencies between human behavior and 3D scenes make accurately predicting human intent a major challenge for human motion prediction (HMP). While recent studies use human gaze to infer intent, gaze signals suffer from ambiguity, drift, and a reliance on eye-tracking hardware. Research in cognitive science indicates that egocentric views inherently encode potential interactions, while head motion serves as a crucial precursor in motion planning. Motivated by this, we propose EgoHMP, a novel framework that uses egocentric views and head motions as robust carriers of interaction intent. By translating this intent into an envisioning of future interactions, EgoHMP achieves precise HMP in 3D scenes. EgoHMP first employs a vision-motion alignment encoder to align the visual and motion feature spaces. Based on these aligned features, an egocentric intent predictor with a modality-aware modulation mechanism adaptively suppresses invalid interaction cues to predict an accurate interaction probability map. Furthermore, to enhance the stability and realism of the generated motions, we propose a contact-aware motion decoder. Specifically, inspired by the human cognitive process of planning trajectories prior to action execution, we first predict a global trajectory to serve as a self-prompt, effectively mitigating the optimization conflict in diffusion models. Subsequently, we introduce a multi-stage denoiser, which relieves motion artifacts by incorporating human contact modeling through a multi-stage optimization strategy. Finally, a GCN-based motion decoder is employed to synthesize physically plausible and semantically consistent motions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EgoHMP achieves state-of-the-art performance.
- 2026-11-10, **FlashMotion: Ultra-Fast Long-Term 3D Motion Generation**, Kaixing Yang et.al., Paper: [@2611.13626](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13626)
> **Abstract:** 3D human motion generation plays a crucial role in game development, extended reality (XR), digital humans, and embodied simulation. Beyond motion generation, practical pipelines include rigging, retargeting, and rendering, making ultra-fast long-term 3D motion generation without compromising quality valuable for real-time interactive applications. Despite promising progress, high-quality long-horizon motion generation under tight inference budgets remains challenging, owing to 2 coupled bottlenecks: (1) generative strategies that require many sampling steps for high-quality 3D motion generation and (2) model architectures with substantial per-step computational and memory costs for long sequences. We present FlashMotion, a co-designed framework for high-quality long-horizon 3D motion generation under a strict inference budget, covering text-to-motion, music-to-dance, and speech-to-gesture tasks. To enhance trajectory-level efficiency, we introduce a 3D motion-space MeanFlow formulation with kinematic and dynamic consistency constraints for few-step generation, together with an Adaptive Soft masking mechanism for temporally coherent motion editing. To improve per-step efficiency, we design a lightweight non-autoregressive BiMamba architecture with condition-guided state selection and AdaIN-based condition fusion mechanism, enhancing cross-modal alignment and generalization to longer motion sequences. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FlashMotion achieves overall state-of-the-art performance in both quality and efficiency across text-to-motion, speech-to-gesture, and music-to-dance generation. The project page is available at https://sun-happy-ykx.github.io/FlashMotion-Project/
- 2026-11-09, **LHNav: Long-Horizon Humanoid Navigation via Multirate Coordination and Physics-Grounded Action Data**, Xueyang Wang et.al., Paper: [@2611.12136](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12136)
> **Abstract:** We study humanoid navigation across multiple destinations under long-horizon language instructions. This task is challenging because robots must maintain progress across successive goals while continuously producing natural, stable whole-body motion through confined spaces. However, task decomposition in existing methods does not by itself ensure progress throughout execution, while waypoint-based navigation does not guarantee natural, route-faithful whole-body motion. Our key idea is to organize navigation into three complementary roles operating at different time scales: a Brain for task reasoning, a Cerebellum for local motion generation, and a Brainstem for whole-body execution. We realize this idea in LHNav, an asynchronous system that uses progress and arrival feedback to coordinate navigation goals, VLA motion-token prediction, and whole-body control. To train the Cerebellum for natural, route-faithful motion, we develop a physics-grounded data engine that improves physical tracking of motion references along collision-aware routes and pairs the resulting demonstrations with rendered visual observations. Together, these designs allow task planning to proceed alongside continuous movement and extend single-goal navigation to successive destinations with natural, route-faithful whole-body motion. In simulation, our navigation VLA outperforms OmniNav and DualVLN in single-goal success and motion smoothness, with fewer collisions and falls. Using a VLA trained only on single-goal demonstrations, LHNav achieves a 75.56% success rate on long-horizon navigation tasks. Deployment on the Unitree G1 further demonstrates single-goal navigation and zero-shot long-horizon task execution in real-world environments, including layouts with repositioned objects. Project website: https://robbyant-research.github.io/LHNav/.
- 2026-11-08, **Kimodo-Policy: Learning Kinematic Action Models for Humanoid Loco-Manipulation**, Yunheng Wang et.al., Paper: [@2611.11554](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11554)
> **Abstract:** Humanoid loco-manipulation requires continuous coordination of high-dimensional whole-body motion to realize task objectives. While vision-language-action models and world action models transfer semantic and visual-dynamics priors to robot control, these priors do not necessarily capture the structure of robot motion. We introduce Kinematic Action Models (KAMs), a paradigm that treats whole-body motion as a direct pretraining target and uses a pretrained motion generator as the primary action-generation backbone. KAMs ground learned temporal and cross-body coordination priors through task conditioning, visual observations, and execution feedback. We present Kimodo-Policy as an initial instantiation, combining a frozen Kimodo text-to-motion backbone with lightweight ControlNet-based visual conditioning. A root-body-hand hierarchy coordinates locomotion and manipulation, while motion-history conditioning and repeated action-chunk prediction bridge motion synthesis and closed-loop control. Following large-scale loco-manipulation pretraining, the policy achieves the highest overall success among compared policies on multi-task HumanoidArena (72.14%), competitive performance on SIMPLE, and 60% mean success across eight real-world tasks, with 678M parameters (92M trainable). Ablations support the contribution of the pretrained motion backbone. Together, these results provide initial evidence for motion generation as a complementary foundation for humanoid action learning.
- 2026-11-08, **InfCoiL: Coordinated Planner-Controller Learning for Closed-Loop Physics-Based Human-Object Interaction**, Yude Zou et.al., Paper: [@2611.11517](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11517)
> **Abstract:** Text- and goal-conditioned human-object interaction (HOI) provides an intuitive interface for specifying high-level intents in physics-based character animation. Mapping such intents to physically plausible, contact-rich behaviors naturally calls for a generative planner and a physics-based controller, yet existing planner-controller systems either execute plans open-loop or close the loop while keeping the planner fixed, so execution feedback never reshapes what is planned. We present InfCoiL, a closed-loop framework for physics-based HOI from coordinated planner-controller learning. InfCoiL couples a morphology-aware flow-matching planner with a unified multi-morphology controller through a shared morphology interface in an autoregressive plan-and-execute loop, and alternately fine-tunes both components with reinforcement learning from the same physical rollouts. To optimize the flow-matching planner online, we introduce Semantic Flow Policy Optimization (SFPO), which stochasticizes selected denoising transitions with channel-wise semantic covariance over heterogeneous HOI features and assigns group-relative, sequence-level rewards from complete closed-loop executions. On held-out OMOMO sequences, InfCoiL outperforms open-loop hierarchical baselines in goal-reaching task success and physical tracking accuracy. Project page: https://infcoil.github.io/
- 2026-11-08, **Does Pose Encoding Matter? Analyzing Geometric Representations for Discrete Motion Vocabulary Learning**, Amir Kimiyaie et.al., Paper: [@2611.11093](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11093)
> **Abstract:** Compressing continuous human motion into a finite vocabulary of discrete tokens is now the first stage of many motion understanding and generation pipelines. Vector quantization partitions a latent space into Voronoi cells around a finite codebook, but the geometry of that latent space is inherited from the input pose encoding, and it is unclear which encoding admits an efficient discretization. We study four encodings that place skeletal motion in different geometric spaces: Gaussian joint heatmaps on the pixel lattice, which we show to be a morphological dilation of the joint point set by a quadratic structuring function; root-relative joint positions in Euclidean space; local joint rotations on $\SO$ through a continuous 6D embedding; and a redundant full kinematic encoding. For each, we train VQ-VAE tokenizers whose codebooks lie on the unit hypersphere, varying codebook size, temporal stride, and code dimension. Beyond reconstruction, we measure codebook utilization and the effective vocabulary size, i.e., the number of equiprobable spherical Voronoi cells with the same entropy as the observed token distribution. Rotation and position encodings keep converting additional codewords into effective vocabulary, whereas the full kinematic encoding saturates at a few hundred effective cells and heatmaps at fewer than sixty. The smallest code dimension gives the highest utilization for three of the four encodings, and the effect of temporal stride depends on the encoding. Encodings of the same pose family can thus yield effective vocabularies differing by an order of magnitude, a difference that reconstruction loss alone does not reveal, which points to input geometry as a first-order design choice in motion tokenization.
- 2026-11-07, **MotionGraphicsBench: Evaluating Motion Graphics Generation with Programmatic Verification**, Jiaju Ma et.al., Paper: [@2611.10592](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10592)
> **Abstract:** Large language models can generate motion graphics from static images and natural language requests, but it remains difficult to evaluate how well models follow requests that combine spatial, appearance, and temporal requirements. We introduce MotionGraphicsBench, an expert-curated benchmark of 100 instances for animating an input static SVG with a motion edit prompt. By supplying all required visual elements, our editing task reflects a designer-like workflow and isolates motion generation from graphic generation. Derived from real-world Lottie motion designs, our benchmark contains diverse combinations of spatio-temporal animation behaviors. Each benchmark instance pairs its model input with a deterministic, human-readable verification program that accepts different valid implementations and reports which prompt requirements pass or fail, rather than comparing against a reference animation or using a VLM judge. We construct these programs with a multi-agent pipeline using human-labeled animations and synthesized test cases. On a balanced test set of 180 animations, our programs achieve 93.3% raw agreement with human judgments, outperforming frontier VLM judges by 4.4%-12.8% while achieving at least 90% recall for both human approvals and rejections. Across 47 configurations from 32 LLMs and 12 providers, pass@1 ranges from 5.0% to 68.0%. Across 15 models at their highest reasoning effort, additional attempts improve every model, but pass@32 still ranges from 38.0% to 95.0%. Our analysis of verification programs reveals that generated animations often fail to match reference poses, follow requested motion trajectories, or coordinate related events. We fine-tune Qwen3.8-27B with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) using our verification programs as rewards, improving pass@1 from 30.31% to 52.19% on held-out instances. Our project page is at https://motion-graphics-bench.github.io/.
- 2026-11-06, **Improving the Diffusability of Motion Tokenizer**, Guanhe Huang et.al., Paper: [@2611.10160](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10160)
> **Abstract:** Latent diffusion models paired with a variational auto-encoder (VAE) tokenizer have shown promising performance for efficient text-driven human motion generation. However, motion latent diffusion suffers from a generation-reconstruction trade-off: simply increasing tokenizer capacity enhances reconstruction but fails to proportionally improve text-conditioned generation quality. We assume this trade-off stems from a spectral mismatch inside the VAE tokenizer: the encoder yields a latent space biased toward high frequencies, and the decoder introduces temporal artifacts. In this paper, we propose the Diffusable Motion Tokenizer (DiMoT) to improve the diffusability of the tokenizer, rather than scaling up the diffusion backbone. To improve text-conditioned optimization, Critic Spectral Adaptation (CSA) leverages a pretrained diffusion model as the critic to suppress high-frequency latent energy. To preserve motion dynamics, the Alpha-Flow Motion Decoder (AMD) performs generative decoding via average-velocity prediction, enabling efficient one-step decoding. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiMoT achieves state-of-the-art performance across various challenging text-to-motion benchmarks, yielding superior motion fidelity and text-motion alignment. Project page: https://githubhgh.github.io/dimot/.

## Vision-Language & Action Models (VLA/VLM)

- 2026-11-19, **PGX: Physically Grounded Execution for Long-Horizon Humanoid Loco-Manipulation**, Kai Lu et.al., Paper: [@2611.26596](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26596)
> **Abstract:** Robotic vision-language-action (VLA) models can execute diverse individual manipulation skills, yet they still struggle with long-horizon loco-manipulation tasks, where temporal structure, skills, and precision are tightly coupled within a monolithic model. We propose Physically Grounded Execution (PGX), a framework that offloads temporal and precision control to two classical training-free components, allowing the VLA to focus on stage-specific skill dynamics. PGX decomposes long-horizon loco-manipulation into three layers: (i) a predicate-based state machine governs task progression, using physical predicates to gate phase transitions, trigger recovery, and coordinate navigation and waiting. Shared predicate semantics connect offline demonstration segmentation, online execution, and stage-wise evaluation; (ii) VLA skills operate within active manipulation phases, with flat or stage-trained models concentrating on task-relevant actions; and (iii) world-coordinate locking (WCL) maintains spatial consistency through base station keeping, object-relative positioning, and world-anchored contact targets. We demonstrate the effectiveness of PGX with extensive experiments conducted on three simulated tasks and one real-world task using a Unitree G1 humanoid robot for long-horizon loco-manipulation, showing improvements in stage progression and task success rates. Project page is available at: https://kl-research.github.io/pgx
- 2026-11-19, **Probing the "Conscious" Mind: Selective Access and Global Workspace in Vision-Language Models**, Jiahao Huang et.al., Paper: [@2611.26590](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26590)
> **Abstract:** Vision-language models (VLMs) integrate visual and textual information, but their outputs do not reveal internal concept representations. Global workspace theory (GWT) proposes that information is selected from parallel brain processes, integrated in a shared workspace, and made available to specialised processes. We introduce the Multimodal Concept Lens (MC-Lens), a multimodal Jacobian lens, to study these functions in VLM concept processing. Its vision encoder and language decoder lenses map intermediate states to a shared semantic space defined by the decoder's output vocabulary to compare concept accessibility across modalities. MC-Lens supports internal concept interventions to examine the role of accessed concepts in downstream computation. (i) Accessibility and selective access. Multimodal concept information is readable in intermediate states and selectively accessible as tasks require. We extend "readout carriers" from one name token to a token set to better capture and express concept information and support counterfactual experiments. (ii) Concepts in downstream computation. Counterfactual experiments show that accessed concept information participates in downstream computation and reasoning. (iii) Development of cross-modal alignment and selective access. We develop two J-space metrics tracking the development of cross-modal concept alignment more clearly than hidden state cosine in controlled VLM training. The experiment also shows that reliable selective access develops later than concept accessibility. (iv) Shared functions across models. Across VLM families and sizes, we find shared patterns of concept accessibility, query-dependent selectivity and cross-task intervention effects despite differences in internal organisation. These findings offer an experimental basis to study GWT in multimodal artificial systems and how it can guide cross-modal alignment improvements.
- 2026-11-19, **PhyDEX: A Benchmark for Physical Reasoning in Vision-Language-Action Models via Dynamic Extrinsic Dexterity**, Hao Vo et.al., Paper: [@2611.26565](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26565)
> **Abstract:** Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have advanced rapidly in language-conditioned robotic manipulation, yet existing benchmarks mainly evaluate gripper--object contact and largely ignore how objects interact with the environment through gravity, friction, inertia, and mass distribution. As a result, it remains unclear whether current policies truly understand physics or merely interpolate from their training data. We address this gap through the lens of dynamic extrinsic dexterity and introduce PhyDEX, a throwing benchmark in which a robot must infer an object's mass, center of mass, and friction to send it to a destination beyond its kinematic workspace. PhyDEX contains 35K trajectories (143 hours) across nine tasks and 100 scenes, covering uniform, irregular, and dynamic-mass objects with static, multiple, and moving destinations. All data are generated by a scalable, fully automatic pipeline that combines grasp generation, motion planning, and closed-loop throw optimization under randomized physical properties. We evaluate three representative VLAs, GR00T N1.7, DynamicVLA, and ForceVLA, and find that all achieve success rates below 3\%. Our analysis reveals two key bottlenecks: limited robustness of pretrained models, which causes most failures before the throw, and physical blindness, as providing ground-truth physical properties roughly doubles success. Experiments on a humanoid robot and in the real world show the same failure patterns. We hope PhyDEX will drive the development of physically grounded robot policies.
- 2026-11-19, **Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation**, Yuzhi Huang et.al., Paper: [@2611.26444](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26444)
> **Abstract:** Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. Yet existing benchmarks either evaluate reasoning and execution within the same tasks or assess them separately without their composition, so they reveal little about whether a failure comes from reasoning, execution, or their composition. We introduce Mind2Act World, a diagnostic benchmark of 30 simulated manipulation tasks from five real-world application domains. Each task has three difficulty levels and two scene settings (Clean and Random), yielding 180 configurations. The tasks form three views: Mind tests decisions based on past observations, Act tests precise and timely execution, and Mind2Act combines both in closed-loop tasks where action outcomes guide later decisions. Comparing the three views shows whether a system lacks a capability or fails to compose capabilities it already has. A diagnosis harness further attributes the failed episodes of three representative systems to Mind, Act, or their composition and summarizes each system in a profile card. Our evaluation of multimodal agents, vision-language-action models (VLAs), and world-action models (WAMs) shows that competence in a single view does not carry over to Mind2Act tasks. Even the strongest agent succeeds in 52.7% of Mind and 39.0% of Act episodes but in only 12.0% of Mind2Act episodes. The strongest agent retains task state but struggles with sustained contact, whereas end-to-end policies execute practiced motions but retain little task state. These findings point to composing reasoning and execution through environmental feedback as the main open problem.
- 2026-11-19, **Risk-Controlled Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Models**, Zixuan Liu et.al., Paper: [@2611.26438](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26438)
> **Abstract:** Retrieval-augmented vision-language-action (VLA) models enable efficient test-time adaptation by leveraging expert demonstrations as additional context without updating model parameters. However, even when retrieved demonstrations are relevant to the current task, the VLA may fail to effectively utilize the retrieved information, ultimately leading to task failure. To address this challenge, we propose R2-VLA, a risk-controlled retrieval-augmented VLA framework that enables reliable test-time adaptation by satisfying a user-specified task success rate requirement with minimal expert assistance. Specifically, R2-VLA learns an uncertainty predictor that estimates the uncertainty of the VLA's predicted actions given the retrieved demonstrations, guiding when to act autonomously or request expert assistance. We further introduce a task-level risk-control procedure that calibrates the uncertainty threshold to satisfy a user-specified task-failure risk requirement. Moreover, R2-VLA continually incorporates expert corrections into its retrieval memory to improve future adaptation while preserving the prescribed task-level risk guarantee. Experiments across three VLA backbones and four LIBERO benchmark suites demonstrate that R2-VLA improves average task success by 9.2--11.2 percentage points, achieving the desired target with fewer expert requests. These results highlight the effectiveness of connecting the usefulness of retrieved demonstrations with task-level risk control to enable successful robotic manipulation.
- 2026-11-19, **Awaken-HI: Scaling Humanoid Intelligence with Whole-Body Human Experience**, EngineAI et.al., Paper: [@2611.26420](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26420)
> **Abstract:** A central goal of humanoid robotics is for robots to perform the everyday work people do in homes and workplaces. Existing egocentric video datasets cover many of these activities but provide little synchronized whole-body motion data for learning loco-manipulation at scale. We build Awaken-Human, which pairs approximately 50,000 hours of egocentric stereo video with synchronized whole-body human motion and covers more than 100 skills. Building on this, we also introduce Awaken-HI (Humanoid Intelligence), a vision-language-action model for humanoid loco-manipulation that jointly predicts original human motion and retargeted robot actions through a shared action expert. We show that loco-manipulation skills transfer more effectively from people to humanoids when human trajectories directly supervise the shared action expert. Increasing human pretraining data reduces held-out robot action prediction error according to an empirical scaling law and raises the mean success rate on downstream real-robot tasks. After full-corpus pretraining and T800 adaptation, Awaken-HI achieves a mean success rate of 93.75% across eight individual tasks and outperforms GR00T N1.7 and $π_{0.5}$ in both individual and long-horizon evaluations. The same policy generalizes to objects unseen during robot post-training and to new pairings of familiar objects and destinations.
- 2026-11-19, **ZeroVLN: Learning to Navigate without Supervised Fine-Tuning of the VLM Brain**, Zixuan Huang et.al., Paper: [@2611.26414](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26414)
> **Abstract:** Representative vision-language navigation (VLN) methods typically couple a vision-language model (VLM) brain with a learnable action head and fine-tune the VLM brain on navigation-domain data to predict geometric goals. However, such adaptation can substantially alter base VLM weights and degrade general visual capabilities. We introduce ZeroVLN, a slow-fast navigation framework that achieves effective navigation with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain. Rather than teaching the brain to predict geometric goals, we equip it with a semantic tool-calling interface for conveying movement decisions to an action expert in natural language. We first adapt the action expert to carry out these movement requests, then jointly improve the brain's movement decisions and the action expert's continuous execution through closed-loop reinforcement learning (RL). Trained on the same navigation episodes, ZeroVLN substantially outperforms representative SFT-based hierarchical VLN methods on standard VLN-CE (continuous environments) benchmarks, while inducing only minor changes to the brain's weights and largely preserving its general capabilities. To our knowledge, ZeroVLN is the first slow-fast VLN framework with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain, and the first to jointly optimize its slow and fast policies through closed-loop agentic RL in VLN-CE.
- 2026-11-19, **TacInno: Teaching Vision-Language-Action Models the Contact Dynamics Beyond Vision and Action**, Weile Li et.al., Paper: [@2611.26405](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26405)
> **Abstract:** Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently been equipped with tactile sensing to perceive visually subtle contact events in contact-rich manipulation. However, adding tactile input yields inconsistent gains, and some visuo-tactile VLA models rely little on tactile input, because much of the tactile change, such as the gel deformation during gripper closure, is predictable from visual observations and executed actions. To address this problem, we introduce TacInno, which isolates the contact dynamics that vision and executed actions cannot explain, termed tactile innovation, and uses tactile innovation only as a training target. Specifically, the TacInno tokenizer, conditioned on visual observations and executed actions, compresses the future tactile change into a discrete code, and the policy, TacInno-VLA, learns to predict the code, adding no module for the code at deployment. TacInno-VLA achieves average success rates of 87.5% across six simulation tasks and 70.5% across four real-world tasks, outperforming the strongest visuo-tactile baseline by 10.7 and 9.0 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies validate the contributions of visual and action conditioning, multi-frame encoding, and auxiliary code prediction. Together, these results demonstrate the effectiveness of tactile innovation as a supervision signal for contact-rich manipulation.
- 2026-11-19, **Beyond the Sink Interpretation: Radial Attention in Flow-Based VLA Policies**, Yuxi Zheng et.al., Paper: [@2611.26385](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26385)
> **Abstract:** Prior work calls the high attention on the first and last action tokens of a flow-based VLA policy a radial action sink, and uses these tokens to set the execution horizon. In language models, a sink is a token with high incoming attention, a small value, and little effect on the output. We test whether that profile holds, not the horizon rule, in one $π_{0.5}$ checkpoint on five RoboCasa tasks. The two endpoints receive 5.4% of action-key mass, 1.4 percentage points above their uniform share, and rotary positional encoding produces this excess: directly in shallow layers and through upstream action-token RoPE deeper in the network. The endpoints do not fit the sink profile. Their value norms stay near the action-token mean in all 18 layers. On 250 paired episodes, masking them or zeroing their values changes success by less than 4 points, and replacing their value directions with those of another observation lowers it by 3.2 to 7.2 points across three donors, whereas the same replacement on two non-endpoint tokens in the executed prefix lowers it by 28.4 points. The token that does fit the sink profile on mass and value norm is the prefix BOS; on this checkpoint, zeroing its value raises success in two paired runs, by +13.2 points (95% CI [+7.0, +19.4]) and +6.0 points ([+0.2, +11.8]). Inside the action chunk, a relative-offset routing profile estimated on other tasks keeps 75% of action-token spread and keeps closed-loop success on three held-out tasks (42.7% against a 38.7% baseline), while uniform or shuffled routing drops success to 3-4%. High attention mass alone does not tell what a token does.
- 2026-11-19, **Alignment Drives Reuse: Learning Generalizable Robot-Use Agents from Heterogeneous Experience**, Ziyue Wang et.al., Paper: [@2611.26376](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26376)
> **Abstract:** Vision-language models (VLMs) demonstrate broad generalization across tasks and domains. However, comparable generalization in robot manipulation remains challenging. Heterogeneity in robot embodiments, task formulations, geometric conventions, and action interfaces fragments interaction experience across benchmarks and hardware. We rethink robot skill learning through alignment: Can heterogeneous interactions across benchmarks and embodiments jointly improve one shared skillset for robot manipulation? We propose UniCraft, a framework for learning robot manipulation skills by aligning heterogeneous settings. Specifically, UniCraft represents observed scene geometry using consistent coordinate conventions and aligns sensing and control interfaces across robots. It organizes tasks from different benchmarks in a canonical format, allowing interaction feedback to jointly refine shared skills and the orchestration policy that guides their selection and composition, without any demonstrations or model weight updates. We interleave tasks from 3 benchmarks and 6 embodiments, collecting 2,700 learning rollouts per agent model. During training, UniCraft improves pooled task success on unseen scenarios and tasks by 9-28 percentage points on LIBERO-PRO, 10.8-13.3 on RoboTwin 2.0, and 3.1-14.4 on RoboCasa365 (Epochs 1-3). After training, with the learned skills frozen, UniCraft demonstrates zero-shot transfer across 4 new robot embodiments and 5 additional agent models on robosuite and RoboDojo.
- 2026-11-19, **ResVision: Spectrum-Matched Resizing and Context-Preserving Inspection for Visual Reasoning**, Guojian Zhan et.al., Paper: [@2611.26318](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26318)
> **Abstract:** Agentic visual reasoning improves vision-language models by selectively enlarging evidence without adding new scene content, but the benefit depends on whether fine structure remains accessible and whether local observations stay grounded in the full scene. We identify three recurring bottlenecks: high-frequency detail can be attenuated during visual-token formation; spatially distributed evidence can remain encoded yet difficult for the language model to retrieve; and isolated crops can weaken whole-detail correspondence through scale and positional mismatch. ResVision addresses these limitations with two complementary components. Spectrum-Matched Resizing (SMR) estimates image complexity and selects an adaptive global sampling scale, avoiding unnecessary enlargement on simple inputs while allocating more visual tokens to detail-rich images. Context-Preserving Inspection (CPI) returns each inspected crop together with the complete working view, preserving local detail and global context across tool turns. We evaluate Direct and Agentic variants on four frontier VLMs and 29 benchmarks spanning six visual-reasoning families. Complete ResVision (Agentic Base plus SMR plus CPI) is best for every model, improving by 3.36 to 4.94 points over Direct Base and 1.87 to 2.94 points over Agentic Base. These results show that adaptive visual sampling and context-preserving inspection address distinct, complementary constraints in multimodal reasoning.
- 2026-11-19, **SKILL.PNG: Learning Agent Skills in Pixel Space**, Maximilian Seeth et.al., Paper: [@2611.26296](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26296)
> **Abstract:** AI agents are commonly specialized for new tasks and domains using skills, i.e. natural language instructions placed in their context. However, the discrete nature of textual skills prevents continuous optimization. Gradient-based approaches such as finetuning, on the other hand, are typically model-specific, preventing skill transfer across different agents. Since many agents today are built on vision-language models (VLMs), we can address these limitations by introducing Skill.png: a method for obtaining skill-like image inputs whose pixels are optimized with gradient descent through the vision encoder of a frozen VLM. Image skills are compatible with any VLM, and a single image can be optimized jointly for an ensemble of models. We show that Skill.png significantly outperforms state-of-the-art textual skills optimized using frontier LLMs in 9 of 16 settings (across 4 VLMs and 4 benchmarks) while requiring only 64 to 256 image tokens instead of roughly 900 to 2000 text tokens at inference time. Hybrid skills, i.e. text and image skills jointly optimized, further improve agent performance. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble. Skill.png thus presents the first agent skill that can be optimized with gradient descent while retaining transfer benefits of text skills. Our code is available at: https://github.com/omseeth/skill.png
- 2026-11-19, **FastRoverVLA: Fast Vision-Language-Action Execution for Off-Road Autonomous Driving**, Changtao Zhao et.al., Paper: [@2611.26242](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26242)
> **Abstract:** Off-road vision-language-action (VLA) models generate scene-conditioned plans, but frequent trajectory updates can repeatedly invoke expensive multimodal inference. Reusing a prediction reduces this demand while leaving its commands exposed to changes in vehicle motion. FastRoverVLA addresses this mismatch by decoupling visual plan generation from motion-conditioned execution. Its training-free interface maintains decoded plans between visual predictions. A speed-curvature action buffer is aligned with measured motion at each update, integrated into a local trajectory, and advanced by consuming the current action. The corrected remainder is written back to the buffer, preserving motion corrections across successive updates. A bounded refresh rule normalizes speed and curvature variations within a newly decoded plan and assigns a short or long reuse budget according to the resulting plan-roughness score. The interface requires neither backbone features nor an additional learned driving policy. Over a 3 s horizon, independent real-request evaluation achieves average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) of 0.328/0.596 m on ORAD-3D and 0.266/0.526 m on RELLIS-3D, reducing FDE by 18.0% and 36.7% at request rates of 6.0% and 4.8%, respectively. These runs retain lower errors across four backbones and both datasets and reduce Qwen3-VL request-processing cost by factors of 16.6 and 20.8 under serial replay, while mechanism and motion-only controls identify persistent state correction as the main source of the observed accuracy benefit. The source code and evaluation materials are publicly available at https://github.com/ADEPT-ORG/FastRoverVLA.
- 2026-11-19, **Teaching Vision-Language-Action Models to Stop Safely**, Zheyuan Liu et.al., Paper: [@2611.26239](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26239)
> **Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) models enable robots to perform diverse tasks from visual observations and language instructions. While prior work has explored task suppression and refusal, stopping prohibited behavior once execution is underway and controlling the resulting physical response remain challenging. Our evaluation reveals that policies which suppress a task from its initial state can still complete it when taking over partway through execution. Even when the task remains unfinished, the robot may drop a held object, making non-completion an incomplete measure of safety. To address these challenges, we introduce State-Aware Fallback Imitation (SAFI) for tasks where holding provides an appropriate alternative response. SAFI comprises two stages, (1) fallback target construction, which assigns stationary arm commands and consistent gripper targets throughout prohibited-task demonstrations, and (2) joint policy adaptation, which learns these targets alongside the original actions of retained tasks. This supervision teaches an explicit alternative response at intermediate execution states while maintaining the robot's other skills. Extensive experiments with OpenVLA-OFT across LIBERO and BadLIBERO tasks evaluate prohibited completion, physical incidents, and retained-task performance. On the evaluated LIBERO tasks, SAFI reduces aggregate target success after takeover to 1.3\%, with no recorded physical incidents or action saturation, while preserving high retained-task success.
- 2026-11-19, **Learning to Think with Images Faithfully via Adaptive Data Selection**, Yu Zhao et.al., Paper: [@2611.26199](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26199)
> **Abstract:** Thinking with images (TwI) empowers vision-language models (VLMs) to overcome perceptual bottlenecks by actively cropping and zooming into fine-grained visual details, promising both higher accuracy and interpretable, region-grounded reasoning. However, our analysis shows that the improved accuracy of TwI models does not guarantee faithful tool use; they frequently invoke tools without actually relying on the zoomed region, undermining the reliability of tool-assisted reasoning. We trace this unfaithful behaviour to the training data: when questions are answerable without zooming, the model can answer correctly while ignoring the returned evidence, and this problem is exacerbated during training as the model becomes better at answering without tools. To keep zooming useful throughout training, we adapt the training data to the model's evolving capability and train on questions where correct zoomed evidence can still help the model answer correctly. This selection is supported by a data synthesis pipeline that localises small objects and constructs verifiable questions about their fine details, and complemented by an adaptiveness reward that favours tool-free answers when zooming is unnecessary. The experimental results show that our method substantially improves reasoning faithfulness and achieves state-of-the-art performance on visual search benchmarks (+11.6% on V*, +7.9% on HR-Bench 4K, and +9.4% on HR-Bench 8K). On multimodal hallucination benchmarks, our model also outperforms the compared TwI baselines. These results suggest that improving TwI faithfulness does not sacrifice accuracy and that both can be learnt from the right training data without directly supervising the reasoning process.
- 2026-11-19, **EmbodyAgent: Post-Training Embodied VLM Agents via Distillation, SFT, and RL**, Wei Dai et.al., Paper: [@2611.26196](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26196)
> **Abstract:** Vision-language models (VLMs) can act as closed-loop robot-use agents (RUAs) by writing and executing control code through tool interfaces. However, turning an open-weight VLM into a high-performing embodied agent requires a complete post-training stack: out-of-the-box open VLMs fail completely at interactive tool contracts, strong frontier teachers are accessible only as black-box endpoints without exposed weights or token probabilities, and supervised imitation alone plateaus under rollout distribution shift. We present EmbodyAgent, an end-to-end post-training framework with three tightly coupled pipelines: (1) a black-box data synthesis pipeline that queries a strong teacher under a forced think-then-code contract and filters trajectories by task success; (2) an SFT pipeline that distills the explanation-and-program targets into an open-weight 9B VLM with both vision and language encoders trainable; and (3) an online RL pipeline that optimizes student-generated episodes via group-relative advantage with a runtime-error-penalized outcome reward. All three pipelines share one primitive-free Python execution tool exposing only raw visual observation and seven-dimensional end-effector control, with no action decoder and no interface shift across stages. Under a strictly separated evaluation protocol on ten LIBERO Spatial tasks (disjoint state splits, greedy test-time decoding, training-only safeguards), held-out success on 100 test states rises from 0/100 (base 9B VLM) to 14/100 (after SFT) and 84/100 (after RL), while training rollout success climbs from 8.4% to 87.7% and median model calls per episode drop from 36 to 11. We further identify a self-reinforcing runtime-error mode induced by outcome-based RL on executable code, and establish a failure-category monitoring and sampling-side curation protocol that stabilizes training.
- 2026-11-19, **RegionAgent: Two-Stage Preference Optimization for Regional Image Restoration**, Weiyu Zhou et.al., Paper: [@2611.26179](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26179)
> **Abstract:** Images under non-uniform coupled degradations require diverse restoration capabilities for different degraded regions. Existing methods employ globally uniform operations, potentially overlooking regional differences and causing incomplete restoration or even damage to image content. Moreover, a degraded region may require successive restoration actions, and earlier actions may alter residual degradation characteristics or introduce artifacts, potentially hindering subsequent restoration. To address these challenges, we propose RegionAgent, an adaptive restoration framework that trains a Vision-Language Model (VLM) through two-stage preference learning to generate a region-specific restoration action at each iteration. Specifically, we first train the VLM to select an action suited to the current region through step-level Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), using preferences derived from immediate restoration feedback to supervise only the differing component in each action pair. To account for the effects of current actions on subsequent restoration, we then apply trajectory-level DPO, using preferences derived from complete trajectory scores to supervise the sole differing action in each trajectory pair. Furthermore, as conventional image-pair datasets do not directly provide preferences over regional restoration decisions, we construct a regional restoration preference dataset comprising 10K action pairs and 20K trajectory pairs for two-stage VLM training. Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate strong restoration performance under both mixed and single degradations. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of two-stage preference learning in improving restoration quality.
- 2026-11-19, **SpatialScout-V2: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore Real-World 3D Scenes and Localize Objects?**, Zhaoxi Zhang et.al., Paper: [@2611.26177](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26177)
> **Abstract:** Spatial intelligence requires agents to acquire visual evidence and integrate partial observations into coherent three-dimensional representations. We introduce SpatialScout-V2, which extends SpatialScout's active mapping task from simulation to real residential scenes through three advances: real visual observations, preservation of original scene content, and interactive exploration of recorded views. Built from 100 homes in Realsee3D, the benchmark covers 3,171 target objects across 29 categories. Given a fixed target inventory, agents autonomously select recorded viewpoints and viewing directions, integrate calibrated RGB observations, and iteratively estimate and revise object centers within a fixed interaction budget. We evaluate the localization accuracy and completeness of object-center maps using mean IoU, IoU-threshold hit rates, and target coverage. Evaluating seven frontier vision-language models (VLMs), we find that six achieve an all-target mean IoU below 20\%. Even the best-performing model, Claude Opus 5.5, reaches only 22.52\%, with just 18.36\% of targets exceeding an IoU of 0.5. Further analysis shows that broad submission coverage can coexist with limited localization accuracy, while target omissions further reduce overall scores. These results highlight substantial challenges in constructing accurate and complete object-center maps through active exploration of real scenes.
- 2026-11-19, **One View, One Action: Spatially Generalizable VLAs through Observation-Aligned Experts and Cross Graph Consistency**, Jinxi Liu et.al., Paper: [@2611.26161](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26161)
> **Abstract:** Multi-view vision-language-action (VLA) models still face challenges in spatial generalization, particularly under changes in camera viewpoints and robot initial states. These models typically receive action supervision only in the robot base frame, requiring them to implicitly learn the spatial correspondence between observations from each view and base-frame actions. We propose One View, One Action (OVOA-VLA), which learns to predict actions in each view's camera frame, allowing the same physical motion to provide diverse, observation-aligned supervision across views. OVOA-VLA jointly trains a base-frame expert and view-specific action experts, whose robot-state inputs and action targets are expressed in their respective camera frames. Two complementary directed attention graphs model both directions of interaction between the base and view-specific experts, jointly leveraging view-specific action supervision to improve base-frame prediction. Cross Graph Consistency further encourages agreement between the two graphs' base-frame predictions for the same sample. With the same multi-view inputs, the model supports both full and base-only inference. Experiments across multiple simulation benchmarks demonstrate improved manipulation performance and robustness to camera-viewpoint and robot-initialization perturbations. Full and base-only inference achieve average success rates of 88.1% and 85.7% on LIBERO-Plus, respectively. In real-world evaluations, base-only inference raises the average success rates from $π_{0.5}$'s 28.3% to 58.3% across three tasks under spatial distribution shifts.
- 2026-11-19, **Better Retrieval, Not Better Binding: Pretraining Exposure in Egocentric Video Retrieval**, Yuqi Guo et.al., Paper: [@2611.26120](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26120)
> **Abstract:** Video-language models (VLMs) achieve strong performance on video-text retrieval and are increasingly used for egocentric video understanding. Yet, despite gains in zero-shot retrieval, they have shown limitations in compositional understanding, and it remains unclear whether they understand compositional relationships between verbs and nouns. In this work, we introduce Ego-ExBind, a compositional diagnostic protocol for analyzing pretraining exposure and verb-noun binding in egocentric video-text retrieval. Ego-ExBind consists of an Exposure Ledger built from the EgoClip pretraining corpus used by EgoVLPv2, which defines Seen Composition, Unseen Composition, and Unseen Atom splits for EPIC-KITCHENS-100 multi-instance retrieval (MIR). We further probe noun and verb binding channels through controlled noun-swap and verb-swap negatives. We find that zero-shot retrieval is positively associated with pair exposure within seen compositions, even after controlling for the marginal frequencies of verbs and nouns. However, higher pair exposure is associated with larger noun-swap margins and slightly smaller verb-swap margins. This asymmetry motivates us to test whether exposure reweighting can improve retrieval with little change in the measured swap margins. To isolate the effect of exposure input, we keep the trained checkpoint fixed and replace only the exposure input at inference. We demonstrate that this exposure reweighting can improve video-text retrieval without stronger verb-noun compositional understanding, and that the gain depends on the correct sample-level exposure correspondence. By separating exposure-driven retrieval gains from verb-noun binding, Ego-ExBind provides a diagnostic framework for evaluating whether future improvements in egocentric MIR reflect compositional understanding rather than pretraining familiarity.
- 2026-11-19, **ChronoPatch: Black-Box Energy-Latency Attacks on Vision-Language Models**, Jiawei Xing et.al., Paper: [@2611.26117](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26117)
> **Abstract:** Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed as generative inference services, where autoregressive decoding makes response length a key determinant of latency and computational cost. This creates an availability risk: adversarial visual inputs can prolong generation and increase serving overhead. Existing energy-latency attacks typically rely on gradients, logits, or token-level feedback, leaving open whether such risks persist when an attacker observes only the final response. We present ChronoPatch, a black-box attack that induces prolonged VLM generation requiring only the final response. It discovers visual delay anchors through response-length-guided evolutionary search, then minimizes perturbation magnitude through subspace-calibrated decision-based optimization. (1) Visual scaling is governed by target occupancy rather than a fixed crop ratio, with full-screen errors typically rescued once the target occupies only 0.32% of the cropped view, while further zooming eventually becomes harmful. (2) Instruction scaling is driven by information that identifies the target rather than description richness itself, as even ground-truth-derived attributes such as color, size, and shape are often neutral or harmful, while appearance and spatial descriptions account for over 90% of the achievable gain. Yet humans and robots differ in morphology and kinematics, so human recordings lack the joint positions and actuator commands that supervise robot policies, providing only retargeted end-effector (EEF) actions. We address both in SHAPE (Supervision from Human Action tokens with Parallel Executable streams), a vision-language-action (VLA) model whose backbone predicts discrete action tokens of EEF chunks as an auxiliary task, while a shared flow-matching expert outputs two executable streams, EEF and joint. On a bimanual robot with 22-DoF hands, a matched study shows that discrete supervision helps learn from human data, driving backbone gradients and shaping action-informative representations across domains. Under standard control pipelines at deployment, direct joint readout excels at fine-grained control with robot demonstrations, scoring 0.80 across six tasks against 0.63 for π0.5, whereas IK-based EEF readout excels at human-to-robot transfer. Pretrained with human data and discrete tokens, SHAPE learns new tasks from human demonstrations without task-specific robot data.
